    "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/for-developers/api/priority-fees": {
      "title": "Priority fees",
      "hash": "c2c30cf2fbdcfc9432a3ea244227179a783bef6b1910a61551f36842c2e5d7fd",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:53:44.163618",
      "content": "Gossip (read) priority\nThere are 3 independent Dutch auctions synced to the same 3 minute schedule. The auction indices are optionally interpreted by nodes as an ordering for their peers when sending data. The priority ordering affects both split client blocks and normal client blocks that include responses. The foundation non-validator will opt into respecting the gossip priority auction ordering.\nThe independent slot auctions are not additive, so an IP bidding multiple slots will be prioritized according to its lowest slot. For the auction to affect latency, the onchain IP must exactly match the IP seen by the peer sending data. Each auction's results only affect the duration of the following auction. For nodes that are connected via a chain of nodes to the validating network, note that each network hop may or may not respect the priority auction depending on how the parent node is configured.\nThe lower auction indices are strictly prioritized over higher indices. The prioritization applies to all data in the mempool transactions stream equally. The ordering should be relatively consistent in expectation but is subject to variance from the p2p consensus network. Gossip priority applies only to reading data. For prioritization of sending data, see the section below on order priority. Nodes respecting gossip priority will use the previous auction winners relative to that node's current execution state. Similar to token, spot, and perp deployment auctions, fees come out of spot balance are burned.\nThe previous auction winners (i.e., current gossip priority ordering) and current auction statuses can be queried using the info request\n{ \"type\": \"gossipPriorityAuctionStatus\"}\n.\nGossip priority reduces the need for market makers to hyper-optimize their infrastructure to remain competitive. To participate in the auction, users send action\n{\"type\": \"gossipPriorityBid\", \"slotId\": 0, \"ip\": \"1.2.3.4\", \"maxGas\": 100000000}\nwhere gas is wei (i.e. units of\n0.00000001\nHYPE) of HYPE charged from spot balance. Each auction resets to 10 times the previous winning price of that slot, and have a minimum price of\n0.1 HYPE\n. Any address may bid on behalf of any IP address.\nThe current empirical effect of gossip priority on mainnet is approximately 20 ms reduction in latency per auction slot.\nOrder (write) priority\nOrder priority reduces the need for market makers to hyper-optimize their order entry and connectivity, and instead focus on the strategy itself. It further protects makers and allows responsiveness to price moves driven by other venues.\nPriority grouping is only supported for order actions where both\nevery order is on a non-outcome asset\nevery order is IOC, or every order is a non-reduce-only ALO\nOrders can be sent with grouping of the form\n{\"p\": 12345}\nwhere the rate is interpreted as a fraction\np / 100000000.0\n. The rate is charged from undelegated staking balance as a fraction of the filled notional in the case of IOC, and resting notional in the case of ALO. The priority gas charged is converted to HYPE using the spot mark price.\nIOC priority\nThe priority rate has a linear effect on end-to-end latency from 0-8bps, i.e.\np = 80000\n. The current empirical effect of order priority fees on mainnet is approximately 45 ms reduction in end-to-end latency per 1 bp of priority fee paid. Even with order priority, all cancels are prioritized before all immediately executable orders. However, higher priority orders are prioritized before lower priority orders as a linear function of priority rate.\nThe max priority rate is 100%. Any priority between 8bps and 100% is treated with identical time preference in the mempool. However, IOC orders with at least 8bps priority fee that would be executed by the block proposer in each 70ms unix time bucket (usually corresponding to 1 block) are sorted in decreasing order of priority fee. In other words, the 0-8bps range effects temporal prioritization, and 8bps-100% range breaks ties among orders in this range received at a similar time. As a concrete example, if the unix milliseconds boundary is\n(T, T + 70ms)\n, the orders with\n(mempool_receive_time, priority)\nequal to\n(T, 8bps)\n,\n(T + 30ms, 9bps)\n,\n(T + 69ms, 10bps)\nwould be sorted in reverse order of receive time.\nAlthough blocks are discrete, order prioritization is continuous with respect to order write priority fee. The mempool transactions are effectively sorted by\neffective_time = arrival_time + f(action, priority_fee)\nwhere\nf\nis a strictly decreasing function of priority fee, and zero for prioritized actions such as cancels. For a given action type, boundaries between blocks are not relevant for relative prioritization, as the transactions within a block are still sorted according to\neffective_time\n.\nOrder priority applies only to sending data. For prioritization of reading data, see the previous section on gossip priority. Similar to gossip priority auctions, order priority fees are burned.\nIOC priority fee paid can be read from the\nuser_fills\nfile written by the nodes as field\npriorityGas\n.\nALO priority\nOn the timescale of\nT = 400ms\n, ALO orders are sorted by priority. More precisely, the tail of the queue at each level consisting of orders placed within the past\nT\nwill be sorted in decreasing order of priority rate.\nAs a consequence, any slot in the queue is eligible for priority fee bidding for a window of duration\nT\n.  Each new order compares against the tail of orders less than\nT\nold at placement time. In other words, the\nT\nwindow is continuous, not bucketed. ALO priority fees are deducted at time of placing the order regardless of whether the order fills.\nUnlike for IOC orders, ALO priority fees do not effect mempool prioritization. ALO orders are processed FIFO as transactions always, with similar end-to-end latency as cancels. The priority fee implementation affects place in queue across transactions. In other words, ALO orders are prioritized similarly in the mempool regardless of their priority, but queue position can change on a 400ms time scale after orders transactions are executed on the L1.\nA concrete example: a new level\nL\nopens and is empty at time\nt\n. The first order\nA\nat level\nL\nis applied onchain (as above, this means executed on the L1) at time\nt' > t\nwith zero priority fee. The next order\nB\nis applied onchain at time\nt' + T - 1ms\nwith priority\np > 0\n. Assuming\nA\nand\nB\nare the only orders at level\nL\n, the ordering at time\nt' + T\nwill have\nB\nin front of\nA\n. A third order\nC\nis applied at time\nt' + T + 1ms\nwith priority\np' > p\n. The ordering at time\nt' + T + 1ms\nis\nB > A > C\n. Even though\nC\nhas higher priority,\nB\nhas already locked in its place in queue because\nA\nhas been on the book for\nT\n, and\nB\nsits before\nA\n.\nGossip priority fees can be read from the\nreplica_cmds\nfiles recorded by nodes. The USDC value of the priority fee amounts can be computed as\norder_price * order_sz * grouping_priority_rate\n. The\norder_book_server\nmaintains the correct level ordering by using the\ninsertBefore\nfield on\nRawBookDiff::New\nfrom the\nraw_book_diff\nfiles.\nPrevious\nOptimizing latency\nNext\nUSDC\nLast updated\n8 days ago"
    },
    "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/for-developers/api/rate-limits-and-user-limits": {
      "title": "Rate limits and user limits",
      "hash": "787b14f52df14e47b388467314b3029d63a8484a465a711b23ad69c0d731bcf5",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:53:45.374116",
      "content": "The following rate limits apply per IP address:\nREST requests share an aggregated weight limit of 1200 per minute.\nAll documented\nexchange\nAPI requests have a weight of\n1 + floor(batch_length / 40)\n. For example, unbatched actions have weight\n1\nand a batched order request of length 79 has weight\n2\n. Here,\nbatch_length\nis the length of the array in the action, e.g. the number of orders in a batched order action.\nThe following\ninfo\nrequests have weight 2:\nl2Book, allMids, clearinghouseState, orderStatus, spotClearinghouseState, exchangeStatus.\nThe following\ninfo\nrequests have weight 60:\nuserRole\n.\nAll other documented\ninfo\nrequests have weight 20.\nThe following\ninfo\nendpoints have an additional rate limit weight per 20 items returned in the response:\nrecentTrades\n,\nhistoricalOrders\n,\nuserFills\n,\nuserFillsByTime\n,\nfundingHistory\n,\nuserFunding\n,\nnonUserFundingUpdates\n,\ntwapHistory\n,\nuserTwapSliceFills\n,\nuserTwapSliceFillsByTime\n,\ndelegatorHistory\n,\ndelegatorRewards\n,\nvalidatorStats\n.\nThe\ncandleSnapshot\ninfo endpoint has an additional rate limit weight per 60 items returned in the response.\nAll\nexplorer\nAPI requests have a weight of 40.\nblockList\nhas an additional rate limit of 1 per block. Note that older blocks which have not been recently queried may be weighted more heavily. For large batch requests, use the S3 bucket instead.\nMaximum of 10 websocket connections\nMaximum of 30 new websocket connections per minute\nMaximum of 1000 websocket subscriptions\nMaximum of 10 unique users across user-specific websocket subscriptions\nMaximum of 2000 messages sent to Hyperliquid per minute across all websocket connections\nMaximum of 100 simultaneous inflight post messages across all websocket connections\nMaximum of 100 EVM JSON-RPC requests per minute for\nrpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm\n. Note that other JSON-RPC providers have more sophisticated rate limiting logic and archive node functionality.\nUse websockets for lowest latency realtime data. See the python SDK for a full-featured example.\nAddress-based limits\nAddress-based limits apply per user, with sub-accounts treated as separate users.\nThe rate limiting logic allows 1 request per 1 USDC traded cumulatively since address inception. For example, with an order value of 100 USDC, this requires a fill rate of 1%. Each address starts with an initial buffer of 10000 requests. When rate limited, an address is allowed one request every 10 seconds. Cancels have cumulative limit\nmin(limit + 100000, limit * 2)\nwhere\nlimit\nis the default limit for other actions. This way, hitting the address-based rate limit still allows open orders to be canceled. Note that this rate limit only applies to actions, not info requests.\nEach user has a default open order limit of 1000 plus one additional order for every 5M USDC of volume, capped at a total of 5000 open orders. When an order is placed with at least 1000 other open orders by the same user, it will be rejected if it is reduce-only or a trigger order.\nDuring high congestion, addresses are limited to use their previous day maker share percentage of the block space. Block space has a constant cost per signature, so batched actions are strictly cheaper to send. The maker share is scaled by the same factor as the volume contribution of that asset towards fee tiers. For example, HIP-3 assets under growth mode count less towards this rate limit. The maker share is computed once per UTC date. During high traffic, it can therefore be helpful to not resend cancels whose results have already been returned via the API. During congestion, the weight of a batched action is proportional both to its batch size and a constant per-action weight. Therefore, batching cancels or orders together will strictly decrease their weighting and reduce rate limit usage.\nBatched Requests\nA batched request with\nn\norders (or cancels) is treated as one request for IP based rate limiting, but as\nn\nrequests for address-based rate limiting.\nPrevious\nSigning\nNext\nActivation gas fee\nLast updated\n7 days ago"
    },
    "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/for-developers/api/signing": {
      "title": "Signing",
      "hash": "698dab00c3d641a21403ab2ed65f084bc34e7263afa5a8a6dbf76805219f39e1",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:53:46.697182",
      "content": "It is recommended to use an existing SDK instead of manually generating signatures. There are many potential ways in which signatures can be wrong. An incorrect signature results in recovering a different signer based on the signature and payload and results in one of the following errors:\n\"L1 error: User or API Wallet 0x0123... does not exist.\"\nMust deposit before performing actions. User: 0x123...\nwhere the returned address does not match the public address of the wallet you are signing with. The returned address also changes for different inputs.\nAn incorrect signature does not indicate why it is incorrect which makes debugging more challenging. To debug this it is recommended to read through the Python SDK carefully and make sure the implementation matches exactly. If that doesn't work, add logging to find where the output diverges.\nSome common errors:\n1. Not realizing that there are two signing schemes (the Python SDK methods are\nsign_l1_action\nvs\nsign_user_signed_action\n).\n2. Not realizing that the order of fields matter for msgpack.\n3. Issues with trailing zeroes on numbers.\n4. Issues with upper case characters in address fields. It is recommended to lowercase any address before signing and sending. Sometimes the field is parsed as bytes, causing it to be lowercased automatically across the network.\n5. Believing that the signature must be correct because calling recover signer locally results in the correct address. The payload for recover signer is constructed based on the action and does not necessarily match.\nPrevious\nError responses\nNext\nRate limits and user limits\nLast updated\n1 year ago"
    },

Integer prices are always allowed, regardless of the number of significant figures. E.g.\n123456\nis a valid price even though\n12345.6\nis not.\nSizes are rounded to the\nszDecimals\nof that asset.     "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/hypercore/aligned-quote-assets": {
      "title": "Aligned quote assets",
      "hash": "280be16fdf7a7fd7bc9b47392b1d18fa9511e8787be27ce36cc29b4ccd73a8f1",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:53:52.193791",
      "content": "The Hyperliquid protocol supports \u201caligned stablecoins\u201d as a permissionless primitive for stablecoin issuers to leverage Hyperliquid\u2019s unique distribution and scale together with the protocol.\nThe motivation behind alignment is to introduce an opt-in setting for new stablecoin teams to bootstrap their network effects and share upside proportionally with the protocol. Similar to the builder-protocol synergy of permissionless spot listings, HIP-3, and builder codes, aligned stablecoins are part of the infrastructure to move all of finance onchain.\nAQAv2\nBased on user and deployer feedback, the AQAv2 spec extends the designation of \u201caligned quote asset\u201d to stablecoins that are not exclusive to Hyperliquid. Under AQAv2, stablecoin deployers share approximately 90% of cost-adjusted reserve yield revenue on their Hyperliquid supply with the protocol.\nAQAv2 will be a requirement for quote assets to be listed against HIP-4 and validator-operated perp markets on a future network upgrade. There is no trading fee or volume contribution benefit to AQAv2. Other quote assets will continue to be supported for other markets, including spot and HIP-3 perps.\nAQAv2 allows specification of a \u201ctechnical deployer\u201d and a \u201ctreasury deployer.\u201d The treasury deployer will designate a treasury address which will share 100% of the AQA rate (the cost-adjusted onchain reference rate oracle) with the protocol through the onchain AQA mechanic. This is twice the rate of revenue share of the existing AQA spec. The treasury deployer must stake 500k HYPE, which is slashable if the treasury address does not have sufficient balance for onchain revenue to be deducted. The technical deployer must stake 500k HYPE and ensure reliable mint, redemption, and cross-chain transfer infrastructure for the aligned quote asset. Both treasury and technical deployers require 6 months minimum notice before ceasing operation, during which their stake is slashable for the commitments above. The activation of AQAv2 is by validator vote, after both deployers have staked and sent the authorization transactions from the staking accounts. Under AQAv2, the linked HyperEVM contract that connects to HyperCore will also rebalance with the treasury address. The HyperEVM balance corresponding to minted HyperCore tokens will be held in a ratio of 9:1 between the treasury address and technical deployer\u2019s linked EVM contract address, respectively. Reserve yield revenue accrues in 30 day intervals based on the block on each UTC date, automatically sent to the Assistance Fund 8 days after each interval completes. In the event that there is insufficient balance to deduct from the system interest address\n0x50...00 + {token_index}\n, the treasury deployer's stake is eligible to be slashed at an interest rate of 2% per day. The 30 day intervals begin on the date of activation.\nAs a concrete example\nCopy\nJan 1: X1 = balance at 0 UTC, RATE1 = AQA-publisher effective rate, published onchain by validators\nJan 2: X2, RATE2 (analogously defined)\n...\nJan 31: X31, RATE31\nFeb 1: X32, RATE32\nFeb 7: X38, RATE38\nFeb 28: X59, RATE59\nMar 1: X60, RATE60\nSystem interest address pays X1 * RATE1 + X2 * RATE2 + ... X30 * RATE30 on Feb 7 at 0 UTC\nSystem interest address pays X31 * RATE31 + ... + X60 * RATE60 on Mar 9 at 0 UTC\nAQAv1\nAQAv1 offers lower trading fees, larger market maker rebates, and higher volume contribution toward fee tiers when used as the quote asset for a spot pair or the collateral asset for HIP-3 perps.\nOnchain requirements:\nEnabled as a permissionless quote token\n800k additional staked HYPE by deployer, meaning a total of 1M staked HYPE including the 200k staked HYPE for the quote token deployment. This is to give builders and users assurance to use the aligned stablecoin.\n50% of the AQA rate flows to the protocol. Validators may vote to update the calculation methodology as regulatory standards evolve. The AQA rate is updated according to an onchain stake-weighted median of validator-reported values. A CoreWriter action allows the deployer to reflect the exact minted balance from HyperEVM directly to HyperCore, allowing a fully automated fee share mechanism as part of L1 execution.\nOffchain requirements, enforced through onchain quorum of validator votes:\nThe stablecoin is 1:1 backed by cash, short-term US treasuries, and tokenized US treasury or money market funds to the extent permitted under applicable regulatory frameworks. Aligned issuers must also provide par redemption at all times, with a publicly disclosed and timely redemption service consistent with their applicable regulatory regime. These conditions can be revisited by the validators, in the spirit of building a regulatorily compliant chain for payments and banking opportunities. The guiding requirement is that a large percentage of the world's circulating dollars could compliantly be converted to the aligned stablecoin in the context of existing businesses and use cases in the financial world.\nThe full supply is natively minted on HyperEVM. Any supply on other chains or offchain must first be minted on HyperEVM as the source chain.\nThe deployer can only deploy assets that directly support the aligned stablecoin. For example, the underlying treasuries could be issued onchain. The net effect is that the deployer must share half of its offchain yield income through the existence of the aligned stablecoin. The deployer and its affiliates may not receive any economic benefits tied to conversion of the aligned stablecoin into another asset. \"Benefit\" includes but is not limited to revenue share, order-flow payments, or any form of rate-linked compensation.\nThe team building an aligned stablecoin must be independent and dedicated to building on Hyperliquid.\nAQAv1 benefits, applied to spot and perp trading:\n20% lower taker fees\n50% larger maker rebates\n20% more volume contribution toward fee tiers\nOffchain conditions are ultimately voted upon by validator quorum, as any such conditions are not able to be reflected directly in protocol execution. Like on most other blockchains, independent validators on Hyperliquid achieve consensus on a self-contained state machine\u2019s execution. This state machine\u2019s evolution is entirely onchain. In the case of the offchain conditions for an aligned stablecoin, this evolution is driven by validator vote.\nPrevious\nPermissionless spot quote assets\nNext\nHyperEVM\nLast updated\n3 months ago"

The API server serves information about this state and also forwards user transactions to the node. The API serves two sources of data: REST and WebSocket.\nWhen user transactions are sent to an API server, they are forwarded to the connected node, which then gossips the transaction as part of the HyperBFT consensus algorithm. Once the transaction has been included in a committed block on the L1, the API server responds to the original request with the execution response from the L1.\nPrevious\nUSDC\nNext\nClearinghouse\nLast updated\n5 months ago"

      "content": "The perps clearinghouse is a component of the execution state on HyperCore. It manages the perps margin state for each address, which includes balance and positions.\nDeposits are first credited to an address's cross margin balance. Positions by default are also opened in cross margin mode. Isolated margin is also supported, which allows users to allocate margin towards a specific position, disassociating the liquidation risk of that position from all other positions.\nThe spot clearinghouse analogously manages spot user state for each address, including token balances and holds.\nPrevious\nAPI servers\nNext\nOracle\nLast updated\n5 months ago"

    "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/hypercore/multi-sig": {
      "title": "Multi-sig",
      "hash": "83248f255930a7439f3d215726498941fcfb6198776a16cbea8039de17982b60",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:53:55.898231",
      "content": "HyperCore supports native multi-sig actions. This allows multiple private keys to control a single account for additional security. Unlike other chains, multi-sig is available as a built-in primitive on HyperCore as opposed to relying on smart contracts.\nThe multi-sig workflow is described below:\nTo convert a user to a multi-sig user, the user sends a\nConvertToMultiSigUser\naction with the authorized users and the minimum number of authorized users required to sign an action. Authorized users must be existing users on Hyperliquid. Once a user has been converted into a multi-sig user, all its actions must be sent via multi-sig.\nTo send an action, each authorized user must sign a payload to produce a signature. A\nMultiSig\naction wraps around any normal action and includes a list of signatures from authorized users.\nThe\nMultiSig\npayload also contains the target multi-sig user and the authorized user who will ultimately send the\nMultiSig\naction to the blockchain. The sender of the final action is also known as the\nleader\n(transaction lead address) of the multi-sig action.\nWhen a multi-sig action is sent, only the nonce set of the authorized user who sent the transaction is validated and updated.\nSimilar to normal actions, the leader can also be an API wallet of an authorized user. In this case, the nonce of the API wallet is checked and updated.\nA multi-sig user's set of authorized users and/or the threshold may be updated by sending a\nMultiSig\naction wrapping a\nConvertToMultiSigUser\naction describing the new state.\nA multi-sig user can be converted back to a normal user by sending a\nConvertToMultiSigUser\nvia multi-sig. In this case, the set of authorized users can be set to empty and conversion to a normal user will be performed.\nMiscellaneous notes:\nThe leader (transaction lead address) must be an authorized user, not the multi-sig account\nEach signature must use the same information, e.g., same nonce, transaction lead address, etc.\nThe leader must collect all signatures before submitting the action\nA user can be a multi-sig user and an authorized user for another multi-sig user at the same time. A user may be an authorized user for multiple multi-sig users. The maximum allowed number of authorized users for a given multi-sig user is 10.\nImportant for HyperEVM users: Converting a user to a multi-sig still leaves the HyperEVM user controllable by the original wallet. CoreWriter does not work for multi-sig users. In general, multi-sig users should not interact with the HyperEVM before or after conversion.\nSee the Python SDK for code examples.\nPrevious\nFor vault depositors (legacy)\nNext\nPermissionless spot quote assets\nLast updated\n5 months ago"
    },
    "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/hypercore/oracle": {
      "title": "Oracle",
      "hash": "1629e301aedb615b3f956af463da825d7750b10cfeea78aafe2e700f14ad12d2",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:53:57.086060",
      "content": "The validators are responsible for publishing spot oracle prices for each perp asset every 3 seconds. The oracle prices are used to compute funding rates. They are also a component in the\nmark price\nwhich is used for margining, liquidations, and triggering TP/SL orders.\nThe spot oracle prices are computed by each validator as the weighted median of Binance, OKX, Bybit, Kraken, Kucoin, Gate IO, MEXC, and Hyperliquid spot mid prices for each asset, with weights 3, 2, 2, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1 respectively. Perps on assets which have primary spot liquidity on Hyperliquid (e.g. HYPE) do not include external sources in the oracle until sufficient liquidity is met. Perps on assets that have primary spot liquidity outside of Hyperliquid (e.g. BTC) do not include Hyperliquid spot prices in the oracle.\nThe final oracle price used by the clearinghouse is the weighted median of each validator's submitted oracle prices, where the validators are weighted by their stake.\nPrevious\nClearinghouse\nNext\nOrder book\nLast updated\n1 year ago"

    "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/hypercore/order-book": {
      "title": "Order book",
      "hash": "c38d99abcdc2747d9fc8fcd843af4c2c933c14f2993e1d0bc0d1218366f4d60c",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:53:58.314238",
      "content": "HyperCore state includes an order book for each asset. The order book works similarly to that of centralized exchanges. Orders are added where price is an integer multiple of the tick size, and size is an integer multiple of lot size. Orders are matched in price-time priority.\nOperations on order books for perp assets take a reference to the clearinghouse, as all positions and margin checks are handled there. Margin checks happen on the opening of a new order, and again for the resting side at the matching of each order. This ensures that the margining system is consistent despite oracle price fluctuations after the resting order is placed.\nOne unique aspect of the Hyperliquid L1 is that the mempool and consensus logic are semantically aware of transactions that interact with HyperCore order books. Within a block, actions are sorted within each consensus batch (there are usually 1-2 consensus batches of actions in a block) as\nActions that do not send GTC or IOC orders to any book\nCancels\nActions that send at least one GTC or IOC\nWithin each category, actions are sorted in the order they were proposed by the block proposer. Modifies are categorized according to the new order they place.\nPrevious\nOracle\nNext\nStaking\nLast updated\n4 months ago"

    "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/hypercore/overview": {
      "title": "Overview",
      "hash": "582ff8f520cb93b5bbc4fd546db49d77c21ce6a50023984cb2e1c1c2b9e55e7f",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:53:59.559081",
      "content": "Consensus\nHyperliquid is secured by HyperBFT, a variant of HotStuff consensus. Like most proof-of-stake chains, blocks are produced by validators in proportion to the native token staked to each validator.\nExecution\nThe Hyperliquid state consists of HyperCore and the general purpose HyperEVM.\nHyperCore includes margin and matching engine state. Importantly, HyperCore does not rely on the crutch of off-chain order books. A core design principle is full decentralization with one consistent order of transactions achieved through HyperBFT consensus.\nLatency\nConsensus currently uses an optimized consensus algorithm called HyperBFT, which is optimized for end-to-end latency. End-to-end latency is measured as duration between sending request and receiving committed response.\nFor an order placed from a geographically co-located client, end-to-end latency has a median 0.2 seconds and 99th percentile 0.9 seconds. This performance allows users to port over automated strategies from other crypto venues with minimal changes and gives retail users instant feedback through the UI.\nThroughput\nMainnet currently supports approximately 200k orders/sec. The current bottleneck is execution. The consensus algorithm and networking stack can scale to millions of orders per second once the execution can keep up. There are plans to further optimize the execution logic once the need arises.\nPrevious\nHyperCore\nNext\nUSDC\nLast updated\n5 months ago"

This gives builders and users some assurance when choosing a quote asset.\nFor any of the conditions above, if there is a three-day period during which the condition is not satisfied for a majority of uniformly spaced 1 second samples, the quote asset will be considered slashable.     "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/hypercore/staking": {
      "title": "Staking",
      "hash": "c97473b8576b71f5676b1f6a03e0dcbc8097910854288422e1e75e3e99ae4101",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:54:02.284349",
      "content": "Basics\nHYPE staking on Hyperliquid happens within HyperCore. Just as USDC can be transferred between perps and spot accounts, HYPE can be transferred between spot and staking accounts.\nWithin the staking account, HYPE may be staked to any number of validators. Here and in other docs,\ndelegate\nand\nstake\nare used interchangeably, as Hyperliquid only supports delegated proof-of-stake.\nEach validator has a self-delegation requirement of 10k HYPE to become active. The self-delegation requirement is locked for one year. Any time that the self-delegation for a validator drops below 10k HYPE, the validator enters undelegate-only mode (i.e., where all future delegations to this validator are disabled), so the validator's total stake can only decrease going forward.\nOnce active, validators produce blocks and receive rewards proportional to their total delegated stake. Validators may charge a commission to their delegators. This commission cannot be increased unless the new commission is less than or equal to 1%. This prevents scenarios where a validator attracts a large amount of stake and then raises the commission significantly to take advantage of unaware stakers.\nDelegations to a particular validator have a lockup duration of 1 day. After this lockup, delegations may be partially or fully undelegated at any time. Undelegated balances instantly reflect in staking account balance.\nTransfers from spot account to staking account are instant. However, transfers from staking account to spot account have a 7 day unstaking queue. Most other proof-of-stake chains have a similar mechanism, which ensures that large-scale consensus attacks are penalized by slashing or social layer mechanisms. There is currently no automatic slashing implemented. Each address may have at most 5 pending withdrawals in the unstaking queue.\nAs an example, if you initiate a staking to spot transfer of 100 HYPE at 08:00:00 UTC on March 11 and a transfer of 50 HYPE at 09:00:00 UTC on March 12, the 100 HYPE transfer will be finalized at 08:00:01 UTC on March 18 and the 50 HYPE transfer will be finalized at 09:00:01 UTC on March 19.\nThe staking reward rate formula is inspired by Ethereum, where the reward rate is inversely proportional to the square root of total HYPE staked. At 400M total HYPE staked, the reward rate is approximately 2.37% per year. Staking rewards come from the future emissions reserve.\nRewards are accrued every minute and distributed to stakers every day. Rewards are redelegated automatically to the staked validator, i.e., compounded. Rewards are based on the minimum balance that a delegator has staked during each staking epoch (100k rounds, as explained below).\nTechnical Details\nThe notion of a\nquorum\nis essential to modern proof-of-stake consensus algorithms such as HyperBFT. A quorum is any set of validators that has more than \u2154 of the total stake in the network. The operating requirement of consensus is that a quorum of stake is honest (non-Byzantine). Therefore it is an essential responsibility of every staker to only delegate to trusted validators.\nHyperBFT consensus proceeds in\nrounds\n, which is a fundamental discrete bundle of transactions along with signatures from a quorum of validators. Each\nround\nmay be\ncommitted\nafter certain conditions are met, after which it is sent to the execution state for processing. A key property of the consensus algorithm is that all honest nodes agree on the ordered list of committed rounds.\nRounds may result in a new execution state block. Execution blocks are indexed by a separate increasing counter called\nheight\n. Height only increments on consensus rounds with at least one transaction.\nThe validator set evolves in epochs of 100k rounds, which is approximately 90 minutes on mainnet. The validators and consensus stakes are static for each staking epoch.\nValidators may vote to jail peers that do not respond with adequate latency or frequency to the consensus messages of the voter. Upon receiving a quorum of jail votes, a validator becomes\njailed\nand no longer participates in consensus. A jailed validator does not produce rewards for its delegators. A validator may unjail itself by diagnosing and fixing the causes, subject to onchain unjailing rate limits. Note that jailing is not the same as slashing, which is reserved for provably malicious behavior such as double-signing blocks at the same round.\nPrevious\nOrder book\nNext\nVaults\nLast updated\n5 months ago"
    },

    "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/hyperliquid-improvement-proposals-hips/hip-1-native-token-standard": {
      "title": "HIP-1: Native token standard",
      "hash": "af71489a52d5ff11b5514fdd9e400107b5f1c940b0eaadaf368a8622598b4021",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:54:07.585233",
      "content": "HIP-1 is a capped supply fungible token standard. It also features onchain spot order books between pairs of HIP-1 tokens.\nThe sender of the token genesis transaction will specify the following:\nname\n: human readable, maximum 6 characters, no uniqueness constraints.\nweiDecimals\n: the conversion rate from the minimal integer unit of the token to a human-interpretable float. For example, ETH on EVM networks has\nweiDecimals = 18\nand BTC on the Bitcoin network has\nweiDecimals = 8\n.\nszDecimals\n: the minimum tradable number of decimals on spot order books. In other words, the lot size of the token on all spot order books will be\n10 ** (weiDecimals - szDecimals)\n. It is required that\nszDecimals + 5 <= weiDecimals\n.\nmaxSupply\n: the maximum and initial supply. The supply may decrease over time due to spot order book fees or future burn mechanisms.\ninitialWei\n: optional genesis balances specified by the sender of the transaction. This could include a multisig treasury, an initial bridge mint, etc.\nanchorTokenWei\n: the sender of the transaction can specify existing HIP-1 tokens to proportionally receive genesis balances.\nhyperliquidityInit\n: parameters for initializing the Hyperliquidity for the USDC spot pair. See HIP-2 section for more details.\nThe deployment transaction of the token will generate a globally unique hash by which the execution logic will index the token.\nGas cost for deployment\nLike all transactions, gas costs will ultimately be paid in the native Hyperliquid token. Currently, the following gas cost is in HYPE.\nThe gas cost of deployment is decided through a Dutch auction with duration 31 hours. In this period, the deployment gas decreases linearly from\ninitial_price\nto\n500 HYPE\n. The initial price is\n500 HYPE\nif the last auction failed to complete, otherwise 2 times the last gas price.\nGenesis to existing anchor token holders are proportional to\nbalance - 1e-6 * anchorTokenMaxSupply\nat the time of the deployed token's genesis. If this value is negative, no genesis tokens are received. In particular, this means genesis holders must hold at least 0.0001% of the anchor token's max supply at genesis to be included in the deployed token's genesis.\nPotential workaround for constraint (2): a small initial USDC gas fee (value TBD) for the initial state update of each\n(address, token)\npair, either through trading or transfer. Further trades and transfers to initialized ledgers are gas free within the standard Hyperliquid fill rate conditions.\nIMPORTANT GAS DETAILS:\nThe only time-sensitive step of the process is the very first step of deploying the token, where the deployer specifies name, szDecimals, and weiDecimals. This step is when the gas is charged and the token is locked in. It is recommended to take all the necessary time after this step to reduce errors. There is no time limit once the gas is paid.\nDeployment is a complex multi-stage process, and it is possible to get in a state where your deployment is stuck. For example, Hyperliquidity and total supply may be incompatible. It is the deployer's responsibility to try the exact deployment on testnet first:\nhttps://app.hyperliquid-testnet.xyz/deploySpot\n. Gas cannot be refunded if the deployment is stuck.\nDeploying existing assets\nOne common deployment pattern is to use HyperCore's onchain spot order books for trading an asset that exists externally. For example, this includes assets bridged from other chains or tokenized RWAs like stablecoins. These deployers often use the HyperEVM for minting in order to leverage battle-tested multichain bridging, including the following options:\nLayerZero:\nhttps://docs.layerzero.network/v2/developers/hyperliquid/hyperliquid-concepts\nAxelar:\nhttps://axelarscan.io/resources/chains\nChainlink:\nhttps://docs.chain.link/ccip/tools-resources/network-specific/hyperliquid-integration-guide\nDebridge:\nhttps://docs.debridge.com/dmp-details/dmp/protocol-overview\nWormhole:\nhttps://wormhole.com/docs/products/messaging/get-started/\nTo deploy a HyperEVM minted ERC-20 token for trading on HyperCore, the deployer must pay the deployment gas cost in the permissionless HIP-1 Dutch auction on HyperCore detailed above. The gas cost pays for order book and HyperCore token states, which are charged to the deployer instead of future users. The ticker is a unique onchain identifier, but as with all onchain data, frontends may display a different name.\nFor the simplest setup, during the genesis step, the deployer can put the max supply (or\n2^64-1\nfor maximum flexibility) in the system address. See\nhere\nfor how system address is determined based on the HyperCore token index. Usually deployers of bridged assets elect not to use Hyperliquidity, which can be configured with the\nnoHyperliquidity\nfield.\nOnce the HyperCore token and HyperEVM ERC-20 address are linked, transfers to the system address on the HyperEVM will reflect in the sender's HyperCore balance, and vice versa. It's highly recommended to test the exact setup on testnet.\nUSDC\nUSDC is currently used for all perps margining. With HIP-1, USDC also becomes a spot token with an atomic transfer between perps and spot wallet USDC. Spot USDC has\nszDecimals = weiDecimals = 8\nto allow for a wide range of HIP-1 token prices.\nSpot trading\nHIP-1 tokens trade on order books parametrized by\nbase\nand\nquote\ntokens, where limit orders are commitments to exchange\nsz * 10 ** (weiDecimalsBase - szDecimalsBase)\nunits of the base token for\npx * sz * 10 ** (weiDecimalsQuote - szDecimalsQuote)\nunits of the quote token. Any HIP-1 token will be initialized with a native spot order book where the quote token is Spot USDC. Trading of arbitrary pairs of native tokens can be enabled in the future.\nTrading fees\nNative spot and perps order books share the same volume-based fee schedule for each address. Fees collected in non-USDC HIP-1 native tokens are sent to the deployer, i.e. the deployer's fee share defaults to 100%. The base token deployer can set this percentage in the range of [0, 100%] but only lower than the previous value afterwards. The portion of base token fees that is not redirected to the deployer is burned. For other quote tokens besides USDC, the fees are sent to the Assistance Fund. Quote token deployers cannot configure a trading fee share.\nFor legacy tokens that were deployed before the deployer fee share was implemented, deployers can increase the fee share once from zero to a positive value. After this one-time change, the fee share can only decrease. The deployer fee share for legacy tokens cannot be set back to exactly zero after being set to a positive value.\nSpot dust conversion\nSpot dusting occurs once a day at 00:00 UTC. All spot balances that are less than 1 lot size with notional value <= 1 USD will be dusted. Here, the notional value is computed as the prevailing mid price of the token against USDC, times the token balance. All users\u2019 dust across a token is aggregated, and a market sell order is automatically submitted to the book. If the aggregate dust is smaller than one lot size, then that dust is burned. Otherwise, the USDC from the successfully converted dust will be allocated back to all dusted users on a weighted basis, where the weighting is equal to the user\u2019s fraction of the aggregate dust.\nDusting will not occur if 1) the book is one-sided or 2) the amount of notional dust is too high such that the book would be impacted by this operation. For PURR, this is 10000 USDC; for all other tokens, this is 3000 USDC. Note: the amount of USDC received may be less than the notional amount computed from the mid because of slippage incurred while dusting or if there was insufficient liquidity to convert the total dust across all users.\nPrevious\nHyperliquid Improvement Proposals (HIPs)\nNext\nHIP-2: Hyperliquidity\nLast updated\n5 months ago"
    },
    "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/hyperliquid-improvement-proposals-hips/hip-2-hyperliquidity": {
      "title": "HIP-2: Hyperliquidity",
      "hash": "1a32f6dc72308c28ae7eecbb1b081fce62f433899c313e09b2a74b1db9966db5",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:54:08.873142",
      "content": "Motivation\nThough HIP-1 is sufficient as a permissionless token standard, in practice it is often crucial to bootstrap liquidity. One of Hyperliquid's core design principles is that liquidity should be democratized. For perps trading, HLP can quote deep and tight liquidity based on CEX perp and spot prices, but a new model is needed for HIP-1 tokens that are in early phases of price discovery.\nHyperliquidity is inspired by Uniswap, while interoperating with a native onchain order book to support sophisticated order book liquidity from end users. HIP-2 is a fully decentralized onchain strategy that is part of Hyperliquid's block transition logic. Unlike conventional automated order book strategies, there are no operators. The strategy logic is secured by the same consensus that operates the order book itself. Note that Hyperliquidity is currently only available on spot pairs against USDC.\nHyperliquidity is parametrized by\nspot\n: a spot order book asset with USDC quote returned by a deployment of HIP-1\nstartPx\n: the initial price of the range\nnOrders\n: the number of orders in the range\norderSz\n: the size of a full order in the range\nnSeededLevels\n: the number of levels that begin as bids instead of asks. Note that for each additional bid level added by incrementing\nnSeededLevels\nthe deployer needs to fund Hyperliquidity with\npx * sz\nworth of USDC. For fixed\nnOrders\n, increasing seeded levels decreases the total supply because it reduces the genesis supply of Hyperliquidity.\nEach Hyperliquidity strategy has a price range defined recursively\npx_0 = startPx\n,\npx_i = round(px_{i-1} * 1.003)\n. The strategy updates on every block where the block time is at least 3 seconds since the previous update block. After each update:\nStrategy targets\nnFull = floor(balance / orderSz)\nfull ask orders and a\nbalance % orderSz\npartial ask order if the partial order is nonzero. To the extent that ALO orders are not rejected, these orders are ensured.\nEach fully filled tranche is modified to an order of size\norderSz\non the side with available balance, with the exception of the single partial order from (1) if it exists.\nThe resulting strategy guarantees a 0.3% spread every 3 seconds. Like smart-contract based pools on general-purpose chains, Hyperliquidity requires no maintenance in the form of user transactions. One key improvement is that Hyperliquidity participates in a general-purpose order book. Active liquidity providers can join in liquidity provision alongside Hyperliquidity at any time, allowing markets to adapt to increasing demand for liquidity.\nPrevious\nHIP-1: Native token standard\nNext\nHIP-3: Builder-deployed perpetuals\nLast updated\n5 months ago"

    "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/hyperliquid-improvement-proposals-hips/hip-3-builder-deployed-perpetuals": {
      "title": "HIP-3: Builder-deployed perpetuals",
      "hash": "640827af890492e6efed56b7aa60909cbc1a2f99c0978768eddd83c1f83aaec8",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:54:10.126795",
      "content": "The Hyperliquid protocol supports permissionless builder-deployed perps (HIP-3), a key milestone toward fully decentralizing the perp listing process.\nThe deployer of a perp market is responsible for\nMarket definition, including the oracle definition and contract specifications\nMarket operation, including setting oracle prices, leverage limits, and settling the market if needed\nHIP-3 inherits the HyperCore stack including its high performance margining and order books. For example, the API to trade HIP-3 perps is unified with other HyperCore actions. To trade HIP-3 assets, the asset ID simply needs to be set using the schema\nhere\n.\nSpec\nThe staking requirement for mainnet will be 500k HYPE. This requirement is expected to decrease over time as the infrastructure matures. Any amount staked above the most recent requirement can be unstaked. The staking requirement is maintained for a minimum of 183 days after the dex is deployed.\nAny deployer that meets the staking requirement can deploy one perp dex. As a reminder, each perp dex features independent margining, order books, and deployer settings. A future upgrade may support multiple dex deployments sharing the same deployer and staking requirement.\nAny quote asset can be used as the collateral asset for a dex. As a reminder, assets that fail to meet the permissionless quote asset requirements will lose quote asset status based on onchain validator vote. Such a vote would also disable perp dexs that use this asset as collateral. A deployer-initiated disabling of quote asset does not disable the corresponding perp dexs.\nHIP-3 deployers are not subject to slashing related to quote assets. On a future upgrade, dexs with disabled quote assets would support migration to a new collateral token. This is not expected to happen on mainnet, as quote token deployers have their separate staking and slashing conditions. In summary, the quote asset choice is important for trading fee and product considerations, but is not an existential risk for HIP-3 deployers.\nThe first 3 assets deployed in any perp dex do not require auction participation. Additional assets go through a Dutch auction with the same hyperparameters (including frequency and minimum price) as the HIP-1 auction. The HIP-3 auction for additional perps is shared across all perp dexs. Future upgrades will support improved ergonomics around reserving assets for time-sensitive future deployments. Deployers also get\n7 + 0.2 * n_auction_deployments\nreserve deployments that can be used at the current auction price, but bypassing the auction timer.\nHIP-3 markets incorporate the usual sources of trading fee discounts, including staking discounts, referral rewards, and aligned collateral discount. HIP-3 deployers can configure an additional fee share between 0-300% (0-100% for growth mode). If the share is above 100%, the protocol fee is also increased to be equal to the deployer fee.\nAligned stablecoin collateral will automatically receive reduced fees once the alignment condition (which is being updated based on user and deployer feedback) is implemented.\nSettlement\nThe deployer may settle an asset using the\nhaltTrading\naction. This cancels all orders and settles positions to the current mark price. The same action can be used to resume trading, effectively recycling the asset. This could be used to list dated contracts without participating in the deployment auction for each new contract.\nOnce all assets are settled, a deployer's required stake is free to be unstaked.\nOracle\nWhile the oracle is completely general at the protocol level, perps make the most mathematical sense when there is a well-defined underlying asset or data feed which is difficult to manipulate and has underlying economic significance. Most price indices are not amenable as perp oracle sources. Deployers should consider edge cases carefully before listing markets, as they are subject to slashing for all listed markets on their DEX.\nSlashing\nNote: in all usages below, \"slashing\" is only in the context of HIP-3.\nTo ensure high quality markets and protect users, deployers must maintain 500k staked HYPE. In the event of malicious market operation, validators have the authority to slash the deployer\u2019s stake by conducting a stake-weighted vote. Even if the deployer has unstaked and initiated a staking withdrawal, the stake is still slashable during the 7-day unstaking queue.\nWhile slashing is ultimately by validator quorum, the protocol guidelines have been distilled from careful testnet analysis, user feedback, and deployer feedback. The guiding principle is that slashing is to prevent behavior that jeopardizes protocol correctness, uptime, or performance. A useful rule of thumb is that any slashable behavior should be accompanied by a bug fix in the protocol implementation. Therefore, HIP-3 should not require slashing in its final state. However, slashing is an important safety mechanism for a practical rollout of this large feature set.\nSlashing is technical and does not distinguish between malicious and incompetent behavior. Relatedly, slashing does not distinguish between\nA deployer that deviates from a well-designed contract spec\nA deployer that faithfully follows a poorly designed contract spec\nA deployer whose private keys are compromised\nThe key factor is the effect of the deployer's actions on the protocol. Note that any bugs discovered are generously covered by the bug bounty program, provided such discoveries meet the terms of that program, including being responsibly disclosed without being exploited. These reports are greatly appreciated.\nEven attempted malicious deployer inputs that do not cause protocol issues are slashable. Similarly, inputs that do cause protocol issues but that are not irregular are not slashable. In particular, bugs under normal operation that are unrelated to the deployer inputs are not within scope of slashing. The interpretation of \"irregular\" inputs is to be determined by validator vote, and includes inputs that exploit edge cases or loopholes that circumvent system limits. All deployer transactions are onchain, and can be independently analyzed by any interested parties.\nSome malicious behavior is valid by protocol definition, but incorrect by certain subjective interpretations. The slashing principle provides that the protocol should not intervene in subjective matters. The motivation is that while proof-of-stake blockchains could hard fork on undesirable state transitions, they very rarely do. Neutrality of the platform is an incredibly important feature to preserve. Relatedly, the slashed stake by the deployer is burned instead of being distributed to affected users. This is again based on proof-of-stake principles and prevents some forms of misaligned incentives between users and deployers. While the protocol layer does not enforce subjective irregularities, the downstream application and social layers can. Ultimately, the deployer's reputation and future success are always at stake.\nThe amount slashed in a given instance is ultimately a stake-weighted median of validator votes. However, as a general guideline, irregular inputs that cause invalid state transitions or prolonged network downtime can be slashed up to 100%. Irregular inputs causing brief network downtime can be partially slashed up to 50%. Invalid inputs that cause network degradation or performance issues can be partially slashed up to 20%.\nLastly, the slashing conditions are independent of the staker composition. Therefore, LST operators should carefully diligence deployers. LST operators should also carefully and clearly communicate slashing risks to their users. A self-bonding requirement for deployers could make sense.\nIn the most likely outcome, slashing never happens on mainnet. A large amount of technical work has gone into making HIP-3 a self-contained and technically robust system. Barring implementation issues, HIP-3 inherits Hyperliquid's carefully designed mathematical solvency guarantees.\nCross margin\nIMPORTANT: Enabling cross margin on an asset is irreversible. Deployers should carefully consider the conditions below before enabling.\nDespite the system-level protection, users still take greater risk when using cross margin across DEXs with different deployers. To better protect users, mainnet validators will enforce that HIP-3 deployers only enable cross margin on assets that satisfy defined eligibility standards, including: sufficient observable liquidity, a reliable external oracle source, and resilience to price manipulation. In particular, each time the `externalPerpPx` of an asset moves more than 50% relative to the start of day price, validators will conduct a review to determine whether the deployer should be slashed due to manipulation. Assets where 50% daily moves are expected more than once a month are ineligible for cross margin and are subject to deployer slashing.\nBackstop liquidator\nEach HIP-3 DEX is associated with a fully onchain strategy at\n0x400..00 + {dex_index}\nthat takes over backstop liquidatable positions from the designated HIP-3 DEX. The onchain backstop liquidators only accept assets where cross margin is enabled, making those assets significantly less likely to experience ADL going forward during high volatility events. Each DEX\u2019s onchain backstop liquidator is an independent user and falls back to ADL to mathematically guarantee solvency of the DEX.\nThere is no action item for users. Once scaled out, the backstop liquidators will absorb undercollateralized positions while simultaneously avoiding bad debt and reducing the need for ADL.\nPrevious\nHIP-2: Hyperliquidity\nNext\nHIP-4: Outcome markets\nLast updated\n3 months ago"

      "title": "HIP-4: Outcome markets",
      "hash": "a8e855458b694875b8634a7e79422d1c7d0cfd206d33f318733a4df6ebadf3a9",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:54:11.439175",
      "content": "Overview\nOutcomes are fully collateralized contracts that settle within a fixed range. HIP-4 is a general-purpose primitive that is useful for applications such as prediction markets and bounded options-like instruments.\nOutcomes bring non-linearity, dated contracts, and an alternative form of derivative trading that does not involve leverage or liquidations. The outcome primitive expands the expressivity of HyperCore, while composing with other primitives such as portfolio margin and the HyperEVM.\nThe first market is a recurring binary outcome that settles daily at 06:00 UTC to the BTC mark price on HyperCore mark prices. See the spec\nhere\n. Multi-outcome markets will be supported but are not part of the initial mainnet release. Additional features and markets will be rolled out in stages.\nThe outcome trading API is similar to spot, with key differences highlighted here:\nhttps://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/for-developers/api/asset-ids\n.\nMechanics\nEach outcome market consists of two sides, each with a token. The tokens are labeled by\nsideSpecs\nin the\noutcomeMeta\ninfo endpoint, often\nYes\nand\nNo\n. Settlement automatically converts either Yes to\nsettleFraction\nquote tokens and No to\n1 - settleFraction\nquote tokens. In particular,\nsettleFraction = 1\nfor \"binary yes\" and\nsettleFraction = 0\nfor \"binary no\" settlement.\nThe order books of Yes and No tokens for the same outcome are merged to share liquidity. For example, an order to buy Yes at price\np\nis equivalent to an order to sell No at price\n1-p\n. Under the merged book, price-time priority generalizes to price-side-time priority. In other words,\nfor orders at the same merged price level\n, the resting sell orders are sorted before all resting buy dual orders. Advanced users may also manually split and merge outcomes to convert between primary and dual balances. See\nhere\nfor API examples.\nMost operations abstract the dual book's liquidity from the user's perspective. However, there are a few examples whose ergonomics will be improved on a future network upgrade. For example, historical orders can return the primary and dual orders separately if a user sends an order that both matches and rests on the book.\nQuestions\nare collections of outcomes where exactly one outcome will settle to Yes, and all others will settle to No. Each outcome trades on a separate order book, but is linked by\nnegate\nand\nmerge\noperations. See\nhere\nfor API examples. In other words, users with No shares on different outcomes of the same question can redeem quote tokens before the underlying outcomes settle.\nFees are currently zero for outcome markets for initial testing. However, builder codes do work the same as normal spot trading, where builders earn builder fees on sell orders that specify their builder code.\nPrevious\nHIP-3: Builder-deployed perpetuals\nNext\nFrontend checks\nLast updated\n5 months ago"

    "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/trading/account-abstraction-modes": {
      "title": "Account abstraction modes",
      "hash": "0e18a533e144beef166c6678ece853e57908cc9aedef3096e019024ac4622733",

      "content": "A user's\naccount abstraction mode\ndetermines how spot and perps balances interact, and whether various assets are used as collateral for perps trading.\nThe supported modes are:\nUnified account (recommended for most users): single balance for each asset. This balance collateralizes all cross margin positions in that asset and is unified with spot balance in that asset. For example, USDC balance is the single source for validator-operated perps, XYZ perps, and spot trading against USDC as a quote asset. USDT spot balance is the single source for CASH perps and spot trading against USDT as a quote asset.\nPortfolio margin (most capital efficient): single portfolio unifying all eligible assets, which are currently HYPE, BTC, USDC, USDT. See\nPortfolio margin\nfor more details.\nManual / Standard (recommended for market makers, high volume automated users, and deployers/builders): separate perp and spot balances, separate DEX balances. Cross margin applies to each DEX separately.\nThere is one more mode that was discontinued, included here for completeness:\nDEX abstraction (discontinued): USDC balances default to perps balance, all other collateral defaults to spot balance. IMPORTANT: Cross margin on HIP-3 DEXs does not behave intuitively for DEX abstraction users.\nImportant details:\nBuilder code addresses must be in standard mode to accrue builder fees\nPortfolio margin and unified account are limited to 50k user actions per day. Standard mode has no such restrictions.\nFor API users, unified account and portfolio margin show all balances and holds in the spot clearinghouse state. Individual perp dex user states are not meaningful.\nSee Python SDK and\nAPI docs\nfor examples on the agent- and user-signed actions for changing account abstraction modes.\nUnified Account Ratio\nTo     "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/trading/auto-deleveraging": {
      "title": "Auto-deleveraging",
      "hash": "5741bcaf3c4f1912e503cb74a9626f5f49a9652486171403db06ff13c3108790",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:54:14.010854",
      "content": "Auto-deleveraging strictly ensures that the platform stays solvent. If a user's account value or isolated position value becomes negative, the users on the opposite side of the position are ranked by unrealized pnl and leverage used. Backstop liquidated positions have no special treatment in the ADL queue logic. The specific sorting index to determine the affected users in profit is\n(mark_price / entry_price) * (notional_position / account_value)\n. Those traders' positions are closed at the previous mark price against the now underwater user, ensuring that the platform has no bad debt.\nAuto-deleveraging is an important final safeguard on the solvency of the platform. There is a strict invariant that under all operations, a user who has no open positions will not socialize any losses of the platform.\nPrevious\nLiquidations\nNext\nFunding\nLast updated\n1 year ago"

    "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/trading/builder-codes": {
      "title": "Builder codes",
      "hash": "aeb8b8b7a4c25f6e9b835523a090394d5bf8378b4ab0779b96b74c5d167f4ea3",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:54:15.144355",
      "content": "Note: The term \"builder\" in the context of builder codes does not refer to block builders within consensus, but rather \"defi builders\" who build applications on Hyperliquid.\nBuilder codes\nallow builders to receive a fee on fills that they send on behalf of a user. They are set per-order for maximal flexibility. The user must approve a maximum builder fee for each builder, and can revoke permissions at any time. Builder codes are processed entirely onchain as part of the fee logic.\nIn order to use builder codes, the end user would first approve a max fee for the builder address via the\nApproveBuilderFee\naction. This action must be signed by the user's main wallet, not an agent/API wallet. The builder must have at least 100 USDC in perps account value and must use standard as the account abstraction mode.\nBuilder codes currently only apply to fees that are collected in the quote or collateral asset. In other words, builder codes do not apply to the buying side of spot trades but apply to both sides of perp trades. Builder fees charged can be at most 0.1% on perps and 1% on spot.\nOnce the authorization is complete, future order actions sent on behalf of the user may include an optional builder parameter:\n{\"b\": address, \"f\": number}\n.\nb\nis the address of the builder and\nf\nis the builder fee to charge in tenths of basis points. I.e. a value of 10 means 1 basis point.\nBuilders can claim fees from builder codes through the usual referral reward claim process.\nEach user can have a maximum of 10 active builder code approvals at a time.\nFor example code see the Python SDK\nhttps://github.com/hyperliquid-dex/hyperliquid-python-sdk/blob/master/examples/basic_builder_fee.py\nAPI for builders\nThe approved maximum builder fee for a user can be queried via an info request\n{\"type\": \"maxBuilderFee\", \"user\": \"0x...\", \"builder\": \"0x...\"}.\nThe total builder fees collected for a builder is part of the referral state response from info request\n{\"type\": \"referral\", \"user\": \"0x...\"}\n.\nThe trades that use a particular builder code are uploaded in compressed LZ4 format to\nhttps://stats-data.hyperliquid.xyz/Mainnet/builder_fills/{builder_address}/{YYYYMMDD}.csv.lz4\ne.g.\nhttps://stats-data.hyperliquid.xyz/Mainnet/builder_fills/0x123.../20241031.csv.lz4\nImportant: Note that these URLs are case sensitive, and require that\nbuilder_address\nbe entirely lowercase.\nPrevious\nSub-accounts\nNext\nPerpetual assets\nLast updated\n5 months ago"

    "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/trading/contract-specifications": {
      "title": "Contract specifications",
      "hash": "de8cb3297bdb625fff320e8725b5f46064acaf0fb3767370ba15de7fd98a8c31",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:54:16.318640",
      "content": "Crypto Perps\nHyperliquid perpetuals are derivatives products without expiration date. Instead, they rely on funding payments to ensure convergence to the underlying spot price over time. See\nFunding\nfor more information.\nHyperliquid has one main style of margining for perpetual contracts: USDC margining, USDT denominated linear contracts. That is, the oracle price is denominated in USDT, but the collateral is USDC. This allows for the best combination of liquidity and accessibility. Note that no conversions with the USDC/USDT exchange rate are applied, so these contracts are technically quanto contracts where USDT pnl is denominated in USDC.\nWhen the spot asset's primary source of liquidity is USDC denominated, the oracle price is denominated in USDC. Currently, the only USDC-denominated perpetual contracts are PURR-USD and HYPE-USD, where the most liquid spot oracle source is Hyperliquid spot.\nHyperliquid's contract specifications are simpler than most platforms. There are few contract-specific details and no address-specific restrictions.\nInstrument type\nLinear perpetual\nContract\n1 unit of underlying spot asset\nUnderlying asset / ticker\nHyperliquid oracle index of underlying spot asset\nInitial margin fraction\n1 / (leverage set by user)\nMaintenance margin fraction\nHalf of maximum initial margin fraction\nMark price\nSee\nhere\nDelivery / expiration\nN/A (funding payments every hour)\nPosition limit\nN/A\nAccount type\nPer-wallet cross or isolated margin\nFunding impact notional\n20000 USDC for BTC and ETH\n6000 USDC for all other assets\nMaximum market order value\n$30,000,000 for max leverage >= 25, $5,000,000 for max leverage in [20, 25), $2,000,000 for max leverage in [10, 20), otherwise $500,000\nMaximum limit order value\n10 * maximum market order value\nRecurring outcomes\nRecurring outcomes are automatically deployed and settled by the protocol on a fixed cadence. The specification for the current instance of the recurring outcome is found in the\ndescription\nfield of\noutcomeMeta\n.\nBinary\nThe target price is computed using a linear interpolation between the mark price updates immediately before and immediately after the settlement timestamp. Precisely, the contract settles to YES if and only if\nmarkPrice0 + (settlementTime - t0) / (t1 - t0) * (markPrice1 - markPrice0) \u2265 targetPrice\nwhere\nt0\nand\nt1\nare the timestamps of mark price updates immediately before and after\nsettlementTime\nMulti-price\nThere are three price buckets depending on the target price at the time of settlement:\n< P1\n,\n[P1, P2)\nand\n\u2265 P2.\nExactly one of the 3 outcomes settles to 1, and the others settle to 0. The target price computation uses the same interpolation as binary markets described above.\nUniqueness\nThere is guaranteed to be at most one recurring series for each\n(seriesType, underlying, period)\ncombination.\nPrevious\nPerpetual assets\nNext\nMargining\nLast updated\n6 months ago"

If validators vote to delist an asset, the perps will settle to the 1 hour time weighted spot oracle price before the scheduled delisting voting time. This is a settlement mechanism used by many centralized exchanges.\nWhen an asset is delisted, all positions are settled and open orders are cancelled. Users who wish to avoid automatic settlement should close their positions beforehand.     "https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/trading/entry-price-and-pnl": {
      "title": "Entry price and pnl",
      "hash": "7054ea5c1fa2bd4a62db070f32024bfd5b1273269035bd2d0980791e5324f250",
      "last_checked": "2026-11-19T08:54:18.416657",
      "content": "On the Hyperliquid DEX, entry price, unrealized pnl, and closed pnl are purely frontend components provided for user convenience. The fundamental accounting is based on margin (balance for spot) and trades.\nPerps\nPerp trades are considered\nopening\nwhen the absolute value of the position increases. In other words, longing when already long or shorting when already short.\nFor opening trades, the entry price is updated to an average of current entry price and trade price weighted by size.\nFor closing trades, the entry price is kept the same.\nUnrealized pnl is defined as\nside * (mark_price - entry_price) * position_size\nwhere\nside = 1\nfor a long position and\nside = -1\nfor a short position\nClosed pnl is\nfee + side * (mark_price - entry_price) * position_size\nfor a closing trade and only the fee for an opening trade.\nSpot\nSpot trades use the same formulas as perps with the following modifications: Spot trades are considered\nopening\nfor buys and\nclosing\nfor sells. Transfers are treated as buys or sells at mark price, and genesis distributions are treated as having entry price at\n10000 USDC\nmarket cap. Note that while 0 is the correct answer as genesis distributions are not bought, it leads to undefined return on equity.\nPre-existing spot balances are assigned an entry price equal to the first trade or send after the feature was enabled around July 3 08:00 UTC.\nPrevious\nTake profit and stop loss orders (TP/SL)\nNext\nSelf-trade prevention\nLast updated\n2 years ago"

On Hyperliquid, fees are entirely directed to the community (HLP, the assistance fund, and deployers). Spot and HIP-3 perp deployers may choose to keep up to 50% of trading fees generated by their deployed assets. The assistance fund uses the system address\n0xfefefefefefefefefefefefefefefefefefefefe\n. It converts trading fees to HYPE in a fully automated manner as part of the L1 execution. HYPE in the assistance fund is burned, removing the tokens permanently from the circulating and total supply.\nGrowth mode for HIP-3 perps\nWhen a deployer activates growth mode, there is a \u226590% reduction on the all-in fees. Rebates and volume contributions will also be \u226590% lower. Growth mode applies on top of other multipliers, such as staking discounts. The following two conditions apply for growth mode:\nThe deployer fee scale must be set between 0 and 10. As a reminder, deployer fee scale is the amount the deployer keeps as a percentage of the user\u2019s fees before applying aligned stablecoin collateral discount, if applicable. For more information, see:\nHIP-3 deployer actions\n. Setting growth mode has a 30 day cooldown per asset.\nThe markets must be entirely disjoint from existing validator-operated perps to prevent parasitic volume. For example, the below are not eligible for growth mode. This list of examples is illustrative and not comprehensive. Similar to delistings, the ultimate decider for disabling ineligible growth mode perps is onchain validator vote.\nCrypto perps against any collateral\nPerps on crypto indexes, ETFs, or other baskets of crypto assets\nPerps on mathematical combinations including crypto assets, such as a linear combination of BTC and another asset\u2019s price\nPerps on vehicles or wrappers that hold primarily crypto assets\nPerps tracking gold price, because PAXG-USDC already tracks gold price\nIn summary, the baseline all-in taker rate under growth mode will be between 0.0045%-0.009% (5-10x lower than the 0.045% baseline fee for validator-operated perps).\nStaking linking\nA \"staking user\" and a \"trading user\" can be linked so that the staking user's HYPE staked can be attributed to the trading user's fees. A few important points to note:\nThe staking user and the trading user must be controlled by the same user. For security purposes, the staking user will be able to unilaterally transfer all funds from the trading user to the staking user's account in a single irreversible transaction. A trading user should therefore never link to a staking user that is controlled by another user.\nA staking user may send EIP-712 signed action with primary type\nHyperliquidTransaction:StakingLinkDisableTradingUser\nand payload\n{tradingUser: String, nonce: Number}\nthat renders the trading user unusable and funds locked. In 1 year after locking, the funds from the trading user are automatically transferred to the staking user. This action is irreversible and not recommended under normal operation.\nLinking is permanent. Unlinking is not supported.\nThe staking user will not receive any staking-related fee discount after being linked.\nLinking requires the trading user to send an action first, and then the staking user to finalize the link. See \"Link Staking\" at app.hyperliquid.xyz/portfolio for details.\nNo action is required if you plan to trade and stake from the same address.\nOutcome tokens\nOutcome trading only charges fees when closing or settling, not when opening outcome positions. Only fee-paying volume is counted for outcome trading. There are 6 cases for outcome trades which are special cases of this logic:\n1. Minting, no one pays fee: no volume is counted\n2a. Normal Trade where one side pays fee: both users get\nfee_paying_px * sz\n2b. Normal Trade where no one pays fee: no volume is counted\n3a. Burning, both sides pay fee: both users get\n(maker_px + taker_px) * sz = 1 * sz\n3b. Burning, only taker pays fee: both users get\ntaker_px * sz\n4. Settlement: each user gets\nsettle_fraction * sz\nOutcome trading does not support rebates. Users who would receive rebates for spot and perp trading pay zero fees on maker orders.\nFee formula for developers\nPrevious\nTrading\nNext\nSub-accounts\nLast updated\n3 months ago"
